ParlaMint-AT_2003-07-10-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00029_d7e6141	Mr President. High house! First of all, I should like to refer to Mr Josef Cap's question: Why this explanation? To say quite openly: Economic and job policy in times like this, as we have a stagnation, although at very high levels, is quite simply a matter of chief importance! [[Abg. Dr. Jarolim: Unfortunately picture!]] This is the case of the head of government. I would therefore like to comment on it before this House and before the Austrian public. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] That's not an unusual thing either. My Social Democratic colleague Gerhard Schröder has chosen the same way in the German Bundestag a few days ago. [[Inter-calls at the SPÖ]] [[Abg. Dr. Fischer: But with the Minister of Finance!]] [[Abg. Dr. Fischer: But he didn't hide the Minister of Finance!]] And it is also useful to take stock at the end of a spring session in which we have made many structural changes: Where are we? And: Where do we want to go in economic and so also in employment policy? The central question on this issue – as we have said, stagnation, although at a very high level – is: Growth. How can we stimulate growth? How can we grow more, but quality, ecological and sustainable? This is, I believe, the crucial point. It is important to recognise that all the structural reforms we have shared in recent weeks and months – at least to a large extent, some unfortunately not! – After a large and interesting discussion process, we decided to have a goal of enabling spaces to grow faster and better in the future. Of course it must be: If we want to set impulses – whether in education, in infrastructure, in the research landscape – then we need to create scope for other priorities to be redefined and re-arranged. That's what we did. All structural reforms serve the sole objective of not saving money, but of freeing our hands for a growth and employment-oriented economic policy. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and on representatives of the Free Trade Unions.]] Sometimes it is quite good to put economic policy in a somewhat larger context. I consider the period 1994 to 2004 to be remarkable and impressive, because it describes the three most important phases that Austria has, in part, carried through and changed spectacularly, and it can be seen that we have had some massive problems in the past, but have dealt with them together and actually impressively. These ten years are divided into three phases: In 1994 the preparation for our own accession, in the years that followed the structural changes, especially in the European Union, liberalisation, the creation of the European Single Market, the euro area in which we have entered, will be a major change in the internal economic policy scene – privatisations, liberalisations – and now the phase of the implementation of enlargement. Ladies and gentlemen! These ten years were a difficult time. These ten years were by no means easy, and there have been a lot of structural breakdowns to be processed at this time. Nevertheless, I believe that the problems were solvable and that Austria today is much better than ten years ago. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] Remember, at that time, 1994, what great concerns we all took seriously; they have not been easily formulated. What has not been said about what could happen on the labour market? And the interesting thing is that today, ten years later, we have 130 000 jobs more than in 1994, the highest number of jobs ever since the beginning of the Second Republic! 3.2 million people have work and bread. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] Something has been very helpful – which is often underestimated in the economic debate: During these two financial periods from 1995 to 1999 and from 2000 to 2006, we have a total of €15 billion from the European Union – that is, over 200 billion schilling! – invest in our regions, in structural policies, in modernising rural areas, in employment plans, in border regions. For the border regions alone, almost a quarter of a billion euros will be available in the coming years, 230 million euros to improve the situation in the border regions. The result is as follows: In the Styrian border regions, for example, there is some increase in employment by 20 percent! But also in the north, in the east of Austria, this once again opened border has had absolutely positive effects. If you look at the integration effects of the Wifo: We have had an increase in growth of about 0.4 percent per year compared to a non-accession scenario. The second phase relates in particular to structural changes within the European Union. Of course, there are, above all, the liberalisations, the changes in the administrative structure, and the interesting thing is that we have some significant changes here. Take, as an example, only the Austrian administration: In 1994, there were 310 000 Federal officials; 1994 establishment plan. Today there are a quarter less, there are 70,000 officials less. However, a part has been distributed – by the way: very successfully distributed – but the total number of public servants has fallen by 14 000. This means that we have been able to deal with much more work with a slimmer and more efficient state and better administration. I would like to express my warm thanks to the public for this great performance. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] We have taken huge privatisation steps at this time. I would like to stress this precisely in view of the previous debate, in which this has actually completely fallen or has been wiped away, in which it has been said that we only want to sell and spoil. Have some speakers really forgotten here in the House – of course some have not been here for so long – that, for example, the former nationalized industry had to be supported by the taxpayer with around 60 billion schilling or 4.3 billion €? [[Abg. Broukal: With your consent!]] And has it really been forgotten – or do you not know, Mr. Broukal? – that in the years 1995 to now we have privatized by a total of €4.5 billion? . We, which we are sitting here, three times as much in our government responsibility as in previous governments. [[Abg. Broukal: I know! I've reported on the fact that you've agreed to this, again and again!]] It was the successful privatisation, ladies and gentlemen: for the benefit of the economy, for the benefit of businesses and, above all, for the benefit of workers employed there. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] We once had to cover the losses – with over €4 billion – now the debt has already fallen by a total of €4 billion. That is to say, the taxpayer has been relieved by this successful privatisation policy, for which we all stand, not only the Minister of Finance, just about this sum that was once inserted. All spectators should also know that we do not just save something here, but deliberately continue a successful path so that the jobs are safe and the headquarters stay in Austria. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] I'm telling you the following warning: Our policy has taken years to build confidence – with markets, with investors, even with the customers of these companies – and such confidence can be very quickly playful. [[Call the Greens.]] Mr. Ollinger did it here. . I would ask you not to permanently frighten investors in Austria even in the admitted economic policy debate. I ask for this as an economic politician who has been able and able to shape things for years! [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and on representatives of the Free Trade Unions.]] We have had a second major impetus in this second phase: major liberalisations. And all these liberalisations that have broken down monopolys that have opened up Schrebergärten for the benefit of consumers were indeed spectacular successes. – This is where the Minister of Economic Affairs, who has made several trade liberalisation liberalisations. The number of trade affected by certain restrictions has been halved in its term of office alone. We have done a great deal of work on this liberalisation in the undertakings. Today, compared with the entry into the European Union, we have 84 000 entrepreneurs more than at the time. 341,000 compared with around 260 000 – a great seal of quality for this policy! [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] We have a 35 percent reduction in industrial electricity and household electricity prices in the field of energy markets – this will become clear when you consider the reduction in grid prices and individual prices. Therefore: This development was not without edges and corners, and it was partly laboriously enforced. But because we have been pioneers within the European Union in some respects, consumers and the economic location of Austria have benefited absolutely. It was the same with the liberalisation of the telecommunications sector. Fixed prices are now 60 percent lower. With mobile phones, with mobile networks, we have a penetration of more than 80 percent and are therefore at the forefront. In addition, we have invested about €4 billion in this new economy, and this has given us a top priority within the European Union. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] Ladies and gentlemen! Again and again it is criticized, sometimes rightly, that there is still more to be done in the infrastructure. I can only point out that in the years since 1994 we have invested €23 billion in rail and road – and this is well-established money. Today Hubert Gorbach is doing with great elan and great visionary goal. Really consider what has flown into the infrastructure of Austria – and what of course does us absolutely well. The same was true of education and research, where an enormous investment boost has been launched over the last ten years. The success story of the colleges is a history of these last ten years, where we have invested 1 billion euros from zero and today have 22,000 places in top-ranking disciplines. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] In schools, huge investments have been made! Under the Minister of Education Gehrer, €1.5 billion – 20 billion Schilling – were only invested in school construction. We have created 32,000 new schools and pupils' places. ParlaMint-AT_2003-07-10-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00029_d7e10170	Mr President. Federal Minister! Mr State Secretary! The Telecommunications Act, which is adopted today, is a new step on a real example of telecommunications in Austria – a path characterized by liberalisation and privatisation and the results achieved. On the example of Deutsche Telekom, it is also particularly close to the human being for each individual, which has brought positive effects on liberalisation and privatisation for citizens, for each individual of you, for every individual of us, and will also bring in the future. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] Over 100 years ago, the first new phone in a locality was a sensation, later there was at least one device in every house, and of us everyone remembers the quarter and the half connections. Then there was a phone in every apartment. It also reminds each and every one of us that one has partly waited for a new connection like a trabi in the GDR. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] At the end of the 1980s, the EU recognised that a major competitive disadvantage in Europe against the already more liberalised American and Japanese markets is caused by the fact that national, mutual and non-standard telecommunications markets do not function and that they need to be opened up to the benefit of people. It has then set up a liberalisation process in Europe, which has started with us the second successful phase of telecommunications in Austria, first with Austria's accession to the European Union and then with the first telecommunications law in 1997, with which telecommunications has broken into a new era. The mobile phone boom has used, and today almost every citizen has a mobile phone, more precisely: Today, 80 percent of all Austrians have a mobile phone, get to enjoy convenient phone calls everywhere and at any time, and that – and this is actually worth noting – at much cheaper rates than before. In the fixed network in Austria, in the Austrian zone, prices per minute fell by 75 percent. An average company – as we know: small companies in Austria! – today averages €8 000 per year in telephone costs – a success of privatisation and liberalisation! [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] The new Austrian companies, whether Austrian consortia or daughters of large foreign companies, have made €2 billion of investment in Austria and Austria each year since 1997. This is a fact for which we must express our appreciation and thanks to these companies, because this is infrastructure investment, as it is always demanded by the Greens – yet they reject this new law! [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] This success story is also marked by the possibility of closer networking for Austrians with the rest of the world. The Internet has also taken its success move through Austria, which has also been invested in infrastructure; more than 60 percent of households in Austria and almost all companies are on the network. This is an unprecedented success story, and it shows how privatisation and liberalisation of markets leads to successes that can be grasped and felt by citizens, in the wallet and in the quality of life! [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] [[Abg. Dr. Niederweiser: Tamagotchi!]] Today, in the middle of the information age, we are launching the third phase of this success story with the new Telecommunications Act. This is the start of the next era – in which we, which I am very pleased with, also accompany the SPÖ – initiated by our Federal Government. On the basis of the positive experience gained, the European Union has recognised the need for further action, and with the new Telecommunications Act, we are implementing five European Union directives which will also make it possible to regulate this market much more targeted in the future. Already in the past, former national Telekom has turned into a successful company. If you need a fixed network connection today and call for lunch there, you say: Does it matter if we only arrive at 4:00? We create fair opportunities with this law for all competitors in this market. Where market intervention is still necessary, the regulator may intervene differentiatedly. We also create a more transparent telecommunications for customers: In future, the billing on demand will be provided in detail. Each customer will be able to take the number if he changes the operator. Ladies and gentlemen! Join us in this new era, for an economic success course for all Austrians, so that they can make even more and more cheap calls in the future and get in touch with more and more other people and with other markets! I would like the Greens to join us. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2003-07-10-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00029_d7e10256	Mr President. Federal Minister! Mr State Secretary! Ladies and gentlemen! With this new Telecommunications Act, we are not only implementing important directives of the European Union, but also laying down new framework conditions for this important sector of our economy, based on the experience of successful telecommunications liberalisation in 1997. The success of the previous liberalisation under the then Minister of Transport Caspar is beyond doubt. In just four years, the price level on the Telekom sector has been reduced by more than half compared to the original year, and at the same time, real value added has increased by more than 60 percent. Ladies and gentlemen! In addition to all the successes that Deutsche Telekom has brought, in particular, with regard to the introduction of new telecommunications technologies and high consumer protection measures and consumer benefits through falling tariffs, there have also been a number of problems in recent years. Mr Federal Minister, here I should like to draw attention to the fact that investments in the fixed network sector have been falling by more than a third since 1999; the continuing trend we are experiencing here threatens the quality of the economy, especially in rural areas. For example, there are also difficulties in introducing new technologies, especially for UMTS investments in mobile radio, through lack of legal certainty. It has already been pointed out: 5 000 signatures are in the form of a petition asking for the limits to be set accordingly. Mr Wurm has just drawn my attention to the fact that it is really high time we accepted this issue, Minister of State. There are a number of unsolved disputes, with procedures taking far too long. It was also overregulated and thereby hindered the introduction of important new technologies. Ladies and gentlemen! In fact, however, the Telecommunications Act is a good example of how a law across party borders can be jointly prepared and finally adopted. [[Abg. Neudeck: Reason has won!]] At the time, a subcommittee was already preparing the best possible solution to the problems. The result of this subcommittee was so that we had two different motions for resolutions, but it finally succeeded in coming to a government proposal – which the Federal Minister and the Secretary of State have worked on – with which we too can very strongly identify ourselves, because a number of demands that we have made have now been incorporated into this proposal. [[Intrusion of the Abg. Parnigoni.]] For example, I should like to mention some of the changes that would otherwise not be contained in this law, namely the departure from the ex-ante rule to an ex-post regulation with a low intervention intensity of the regulator – let it play more market, the proportionality on the market has now changed better – and the maintenance of the existing government structure, each of which is a regulator for the telecom sector, i.e. for the economy, and a regulator for the media sector. We have also insisted on the continuation of the universal service in its existing scope and its qualitative development, further incentives to increase investment in the telecoms sector, especially incentives for the promotion of broadband Internet in Austria. Enforcement of telephone numbers portability means nothing but that you can now switch to any provider with the same number you have if it has a cheaper range of offers. We have also enforced the strengthening of consumer rights, which is a very essential point in this area. A two-year telecommunications report will also be available to Parliament in the future. It can be said that two years are long, or that is not as long as Mr Moser meant earlier, but one thing is certain: It is now true that, in principle, we are getting this report at all, and that is positive! [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] This enables the optimal development of the Telekom sector and thus of Austria's economic location. However, there were still some wishes in the area of consumer protection. In this regard, we have been negotiating with the ladies and gentlemen of the government coalition for a very long time, and after these difficult negotiations we have finally succeeded in finding a good solution. For example, there will be a cost-effective restriction on value added services as well as mandatory consumer price information, with the aim of limiting the cost of 0800 numbers and 0800 services by Regulation to a maximum of 22 cents per minute. The Federal Minister has promised that he will make a regulation – and he will now nod to it – that 22 cents per minute will be considered as the upper limit. Furthermore, it will be possible in the future to have telephone numbers blocked for free once a year. This is important in terms of consumer protection. Let's think of the 0900 numbers, where it can often be such that anyone uses devices, causes high bills, and the holder, the payer of these numbers eventually gets mint and it comes out that anyone else has caused high costs over his numbers. We have also insisted that it should not be possible, through so-called dialer programs on the Internet, to be re-directed to telephone numbers with enormous costs, because one must know that there are technically numerous new ways to get more money out of the consumer's pockets. Finally, we have achieved that the regulatory authority has to inform the House annually, in particular, of the unfair practices and the measures to be taken or taken. This will make it possible for us to substantially limit the constantly re-enacting fraudulent measures. I also do not want to thank Mr Gorbach and his colleague Kukacka, who have responded to the SPÖ's arguments and who in fact made these important concerns of consumer protection a law. With this new Telecommunication Act, Austria will receive the framework conditions which will increase the investment willingness of Deutsche Telekom companies. This has been an important concern for us, and it will always be ours. However, I can draw attention to one thing: Even if we now agree, the government will not make a serious mistake in the future. We believe that it is wrong to sell Deutsche Telekom Austria to abroad, for example to Swisscom, as it was now in talk. This is wrong, therefore, because we need an important national market player here to help shape the conditions on the Telekom sector, secure and promote investment and thus jobs, in the end also in order to be able to take an entrepreneurial view of the price situation for Telekom services and the service in Austria. Ladies and gentlemen, therefore here the request to deal with this change of ownership very carefully! [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] As far as the Telekom law itself is concerned, I consider it a good, successful law. This law will enable us to promote high tech, promote jobs, secure business location quality and maintain consumer protection at a high level. - Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-AT_2003-07-10-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00029_d7e10923	Mr President. Federal Minister! Mr. State Secretary! Ladies and gentlemen! High house! The need for adaptation of the Telecommunications Act results on the one hand from the so-called Telekom Reform Package of the Council of the European Union, which provides for the transposition of the relevant Directives into national law. On the other hand, technically high-quality and economically efficient telecommunications networks are now the basic prerequisite for a good economic location. After opening up the telecommunications market for private providers, they tried to position their competitive advantage over optimal networks, which in the past has led to a true wild growth in transmission masts. Partnership solutions in the technical field among competitors had not previously been given. The term Site-sharing was actually a foreign word. This has had an impact on the population’s displeasure and misunderstanding among the local authorities. In the meantime, competition for the customer has significantly shifted. Today, there are services that are crucial: From the e-mail via sent photos, remote control, remote effect up to SMS, the offer extends. The new Telecommunications Act provides for co-use rights under § 8. In the future, owners or authorized users of antenna masts or power lines will allow the use of firearms, rescue services and security authorities. Broadband technology is a must for universities, schools, businesses and many private ones. In order to be able to implement this technology quickly, it is necessary that a broad offer is made. This is why § 14 provides: ‘Every man is entitled to provide communications networks and services in compliance with the legal provisions.’ With regard to wired technology such as wiring in the above and underground form, close cooperation with the municipalities is essential in order to enable synergies with the canal, water supply and district heating construction. Ladies and gentlemen! I am very glad that our head of state Dr. Erwin Pröll and our economic councillor Ernest Gabmann jointly launched the initiative “Breitband Niederösterreich”. This initiative will ensure that we can make this technology available within a year at the latest, and the term “virtual village” will be given a positive added meaning. Ladies and gentlemen! It is a modern, a good law. I'm asking for your consent.

ParlaMint-AT_2003-07-10-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00029_d7e10993	Mr President. Government members! Ladies and gentlemen! We are at the threshold of a new Austrian Telekom future: The development of an open market in telecommunications is practically complete. Now it is important to set sustainable impetus so that Austria can be successful as an innovative Telekom infrastructure country and as a driving force for the Telekom employment market. Special attention must be paid to rural areas which are not as attractive to private investors due to the lack of mass as the agglomerations. It will therefore also require special funding in this area, for example on the issue of Internet broadband connections. Following a comparatively early market launch of broadband, Austria has fallen to fifth place in Europe last year. In order for Austria to regain a leading position among the information societies of Europe, targeted national efforts in broadband access technologies must be made. It is also necessary to strengthen demand with useful e-commerce solutions and e-government products. Although Austria is obliged by the E-Europe Action Plan to achieve comprehensive supply by 2005, there are currently no quantitative targets and no time horizons regarding the extension of broadband access in rural areas. Nor is there any concept or strategy in this area of competitiveness so central. Therefore, we call for direct support, comprehensive supply of sufficient capacity, comprehensive equipment of schools, training for all groups of people and increased use of existing and alternative funding lines in order to strengthen rural areas. In the spring, the broadcasting and telecom regulation company launched the Austrian broadband initiative. Based on an analysis of the status quo, this society should, in accordance with the motto “Broadband for All”, identify, inter alia, options for promoting broadband penetration, especially for sub- and unsupply areas, and develop the appropriate models for the public, Parliament and the government. In particular, the aim of the initiative should be to coordinate and manage Austrian activities to improve broadband provision. The law we now have, ladies and gentlemen, unfortunately does not contain any of this. The only measure adopted by the Government is not appropriate to produce sufficient impact in the less favoured regions. Ladies and gentlemen! A great opportunity for rural areas has been denied.

ParlaMint-AT_2003-07-10-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00029_d7e11054	Mr President. Federal Minister! Mr State Secretary! High house! The Telecommunications Act 2003 in its recast, of course, contains a number of innovations that have become necessary because the Council of the European Union has essentially issued four directives. I would like to take up an area that is particularly interesting for consumers, because this Telekom law is also very relevant to industry-internal areas. One area is very important for consumers – this has already been mentioned by the Federal Minister – because in future mobile users will have the opportunity to keep their telephone numbers. The technically restricted term ‘number portability’ means nothing other than that someone who likes his number very well, or who wants to have his phone number, for professional or any reason, can continue to keep his telephone number under the same pre-election. This is, of course, a hot topic for operators in this billion-dollar market. Considering that the so-called market penetration actually means that 80 percent of the Austrians already have a mobile device from kindergarten to the retirement home and have developed into a billion-dollar market, of course the change that offers a market shift is of particular importance to the operators. The current market share is roughly as follows: Mobilkom has 44.7 percent of the market share, T-Mobile has 29.6 percent, One about 20.3 percent, and Tele.ring 5.4 percent. Now, of course, a new regulation by the European Union, which wants it to be transformed into national law, makes a significant shift in the market possible. And that is why there was also a great doubling between those who are new to the market and those who naturally want to keep their market share. All in all, however, this is especially beneficial for consumers. Yesterday – Mr Gorbach has already mentioned this – an agreement was reached, which at first seemed impossible, namely an agreement between mobile operators in relation to this telephone number portability. Just briefly: If you want to change, you just need to go to the new operator, but don't log out with the old one. So what we know from advertising, what we know from the banking sector, is now also available to mobile operators: If you want to switch to a new one, you just need to go there more. In a standardized contract, the customer of course also experiences the terms and conditions he or she enters into. Technically, this is how it works: A change may take a maximum of three days – a relatively long period at a time of technologicalization, at a time of telecommunications. However, it must be borne in mind that it is only possible to change at night, because the networks are very well stocked during the day, and it must also be borne in mind that there may be a weekend between them. However, the fact is that this is likely to be done in about one day at the latest. The cost of the change is to move in the ‘surmountable framework’; this has not been precisely defined. An example is: In Germany, the changeover pays; the costs for this amount to approximately € 20 to 25; however, it is still charged exactly what a market-friendly price is. – It is to be learned from operators here in Austria that one will at least try to take over some of these costs for those who want to change. In addition, there will be a uniform text message on which network you are calling, because that is important. It may well be that the person you call has suddenly changed the network, but at the first moment you cannot see it, because the phone number has just remained the same. The customer will then be able to decide whether he wants this text message or not. All the new regulations, therefore, ladies and gentlemen, which are for the benefit of mobile users and consumer-friendly. I am particularly pleased that a consensus with the Social Democrats could be found in this difficult matter, with which this important law is widely approved. It is important that many tasks are done with the TKG 2003 as it were, e.g. competition regulation, a periodic recurring evaluation rather than an ex-ante regulation; et cetera. It is therefore a flexible system. All in all: a new, a good law, which thank God is also given broad approval.

ParlaMint-AT_2003-07-10-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00029_d7e12521	Mr President. Minister! Mr State Secretary! Ladies and gentlemen! The European Parliament and the Council have adopted a further liberalisation of the postal market. The monopoly for mail will therefore become from 1st. January 2006 lowered to 50 grams on mail. ==References== In 2003, the monopoly was lowered from 350 grams to 100 grams. This liberalisation will create a fair market, and fair conditions of competition will be created – the consumer will ultimately benefit from it. A significant part of this innovation concerns the new legal basis for letter-processing facilities. There have so far been no binding rules on design and equipment. Most of the facilities are lockable and not accessible from the outside. They do not have a slot and are owned by Austrian Post AG. Only the post has access to it. In Austria, 1.7 million mailboxes are installed and 1.1 million of them do not have public access. – This represents an uneven competition for me personally. It is not possible for other service providers to use these subjects. With the new regulation, we will create equal competition. [[Abg. Gradwell: And what if I don't want it?]] Mr Gradwelt. — (DE) Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I should like to thank Of course, this is also associated with costs, of course someone has to pay, but the amount of costs does not change, whether you implement it until 2006 or until 2009. Gradwell: What if I don't want it as a property owner?]] Ladies and gentlemen! I know that the deadline for 2009 has been set by the EU – this is a long period in which we can move – but we, the majority of governments, have decided that we will implement these changes by 2006. Of course, it is unfortunate that costs for homeowners are incurred. However, there is a need for a level playing field in the field of competition. More for consumers, and these benefits are our personal priority. That is why we have also given our assent. ParlaMint-AT_2003-07-10-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00029_d7e12871	Mr Dipl.-Ing. Controller. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2003-07-10-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00029_d7e12890	Mr President. Federal Minister! Mr State Secretary! High house! We have a whole package of aviation arrangements to negotiate today, and I should like to speak now on all points. I would like to start with the Convention on International Aviation, the so-called Montreal Agreement, which was concluded in May 1999. For years negotiations have been conducted, Austrian experts have been involved, and I think we have achieved a good result. So far, there have been the 1929 Warsaw Convention, to which there have been four protocols of amendment. Everything is now regulated in a modern way, in particular the transport documents, but also the liability for delays, for damage to persons, luggage and air freight. A arbitration procedure is provided for, the court is set out et cetera. Since there are already 71 signatar states, we really have a modern agreement for international aviation, and we can only say: Adieu Warsaw, bienvenue Montreal! Second point, the novel to the Aviation Act. – This has been the first major innovation since 1997. On the one hand, it concerns the implementation of regulations from EU regulations, for example the allocation of slots at airports. But above all, it is about implementing regulations of the Joint Aviation Authorities, the so-called Jars, the Joint Aviation Regulations, which need to be implemented. I think it is a good way that we have many regulatory powers for the Minister of Transport who can declare these years binding. It is about as important things as the review of airworthiness, the qualification of personnel and training establishments or the requirements for maintenance companies – all things that serve aviation safety. In this context, I would like to address briefly the costs of aviation, both for carriers and for airports. In fact, it lays down the costs of the use of air traffic control services in accordance with the aviation law of the Transport Ministers, and I would sincerely ask that in the interests of carriers and consumers, there be great cost-effectiveness and not all kinds of costs are taken into account. The same is true, Mr Federal Minister, of the security services at airports, which are also charged to consumers. However, public security is also an essential public task, which is why I would ask you to pay attention to the best possible cost in this area. Everything at the expense of the companies must be calculated on the passenger's own account and thus affords to fly. It is of course – and I fully agree – that absolute security must be given, but a cost-effectiveness should always take place. I would also like to say a few words about what will be negotiated under item 14 on which I am the rapporteur. This is about the ceatS agreement. We currently have about 55 air traffic control centres, national headquarters in Europe, and these are now to be merged into seven areas in Europe. Eight states of the South-East European region have joined forces with the Centre of Austria and now want to operate air traffic control together, which is to be transferred to Eurocontrol. It is about the upper airspace at an altitude of more than 8,600 meters, where the majority of the civil aviation takes place. This merger is expected to lead to synergies; we hope to reduce air navigation charges by 20 percent by 2010. It is intended to eliminate delays, to increase safety by removing interfaces when passing from one surveillance point to the other, and to increase the efficiency of the airways. It is particularly positive to mention that this air traffic control centre will have its headquarters in Austria, namely in Fischamend. We had first offered a location in Vienna on the overplasing of the Danube River Autobahn, in fact the headquarters will now settle in Fischamend because an airport proximity is much cheaper in terms of safety et cetera. This means that 400 CEATS officials, 400 employees, will work there for us. We had great competition through Prague and Budapest. I would like to congratulate the negotiators – these are the Ministry of Transport and the Ministry of Foreign Affairs – on this very great success, that the headquarters of the air traffic control centre will be in Austria throughout the south-eastern European region, and that we have enforced ourselves there against the strong competition from other countries.

It should be noted that the ÖIAG Act does not provide that the Board of Directors or the Supervisory Board should be informed of the owner's representatives during the period of investigation. The law clearly requires that a privatisation programme and then a privatisation concept be drawn up by the Executive Board for each company, and that is to be decided by the Supervisory Board. ParlaMint-AT_2003-07-10-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00029_d7e23781	Mr President. Mr State Secretary! Ladies and gentlemen! The European Union has removed many borders on the ground, but the EU has so far been quite powerless to deliver national interests in the sky. European airspace surveillance is characterised by a skewer species dimension; airspace monitoring has so far been regarded as a national matter. Europe has around 60 national air control centres with several hundred interfaces along the air routes. In the effort to establish a single airspace surveillance, after years of preparation, the principle agreement was reached in 1997 that there should be more than 28 500 feet of joint air traffic control in the Central European airspace. [[President Dr. Khol shall take over the chairmanship.]] This set the course for the CEATS project. The State Treaty for the Far-Aeronautical Convention CEATS establishes, among other things, a joint air navigation centre for Central Europe in Fischamend. This further upgrades Austria's location to an important European organization and makes Vienna an important hub of international air traffic. Ladies and gentlemen! Between the first initiatives to implement CEATS 92 and the actual start of the CEATS Centre in Austria 2010 is now a period of 17 years. In these 17 years, European air traffic has developed rapidly. Aviation has grown the fastest of all modes of transport – we know – over the last 20 years and will increase by 4 percent per year. As the European Commission notes on its website, the airspace is organised as in the 1960s. A simple flight from Rome to Brussels crosses nine different control centres. The Single European Sky initiative was also launched in the previous year. This is specifically about bringing the obsolete architecture of European airspace surveillance to the fore, making the airspace optimal according to functional criteria and improving aviation security systems. The CEATS airspace could become one of these “Functional Blocks of Airspace” in a single European airspace. CEATS is currently being discussed on the need for adaptation due to its long lead-up period by the EU Single European Sky initiative. At the end of the day, I can only give a hint: With all due regard, we should all try to ensure that aviation safety standards are maintained or improved. - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2003-07-10-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00029_d7e24045	Mr President. Thank you, Mrs. Minister! Mr. State Secretary! Ladies and gentlemen, In the early 1990s, the discussion on the further development and codification of patient rights began. An analysis of the situation showed that the situation of this type of patient right is spread across several laws, both in federal and national law. There is a lack of information for those concerned and also a lack of enforceability. All these problems led to the consideration not to create its own patient rights law, but to the attempt to give, on the basis of an agreement under Article 15a of the Federal Constitutional Law, a complete and clear summary of all patient rights, that is, a patient charter, to safeguard the patient's rights within the framework of their competence. The present agreement with the federal state of Tyrol contains rules on the following key areas of patient rights: Right to treatment and care, right to respect for dignity and integrity, right to self-determination and Information, right to documentation, special provision for children, representation of patient interests and enforcement of claims for damages. I would like to take care of this very briefly. In order to create a familiar environment for long-term patients, for example, it will be possible to take away personal items and attach images or other commemoratives, which is already happening largely in hospitals or in the extramural area, i.e. nursing homes et cetera. The circumstances of dying in the hospital are particularly stressful for both deaths and family members in many cases. It is necessary to design the organisational structures in hospitals in such a way that farewell is possible in a humane, decent environment. I am particularly concerned about the stationary treatment of unmistakable minors up to the tenth year of life. It is therefore necessary to provide for the possibility of including accompanying persons. I think that's very important. Thank you.

These products are usually distributed through mailbox companies. That is, people have to pay this in advance; if it does not, they have spent money on it and the “klumper ” is at home. I think you really have to look at that more closely. It is also necessary to oppose advertising quite clearly and to regulate it in such a way that these companies – in this case companies – are simply punished accordingly, because they do more harm to the people than benefits. Austria has also not become the master of this problem. For example, if I look at the television advertising, sometimes I wonder when any drinking products are shown and then it is written: The positive effect is confirmed by the Ministry of Health. The fact is, that in the case of drinking products – I can't now call the company – there are: The positive effect of this drink has been officially confirmed by the Ministry of Health. But if this is the case, which is said on television and so passed on to consumers, then every citizen, I say now, usually assume that this is really a drug, because medicines that are not authorised in Austria must not be sold either. This is a very delicate and dangerous development, which is just as dangerous – and that has already been addressed today – as is the whole problem with magnetic field therapy. I also know people who swear on it and lie on such a mat. You feel, I think, no matter if it's turned on or not, it just has a very psychological and not necessarily a healing effect. It bothers me very much when I roll in medical practices when there are huge posters on which magnetic field therapy with all its supposed positive effects, as it is in advertising, is applied in the medical practice. It says that this helps against cancer and has a positive effect on a number of diseases. Imagine the following: If a patient goes to the doctor today, he assumes that everything that is in practice on the wall is in his interest and the doctor can recommend it more or less positively, because something negative will probably not hang in his practice. And I know from reliable sources that doctors also benefit financially from the sale of these magnetic field mats. I think it has to be stopped once. Mrs Minister, I've only taken out a few small areas now, but I think there's a lot of work going on to you. We must react very quickly because the health and illness of the population are driving a lot of schindluder. And that always goes – really always! – only at the expense of those who are already sick or at risk of their health. You can't do that, but you just have to counter it. I would like to say a few more words about the Health and Hospital Care Act. I think it is positive that now the up-to-date medical service can implement life-saving emergency measures, that this is finally regulated. Especially the people who work in the medical service are usually quite simple those who are the first to be with the patient. What they can do there in advance, in positive performance, usually has a very intense impact on the consequences of the disease. I am thinking, for example, of stroke patients. I think it's positive that this is being settled well. I also think it is in principle good that there should be the possibility of subdelegation from now on. But, Mrs Minister, what I am missing is that the staff who can carry out this activity, namely the nursing staff, is then upgraded accordingly. It is supposed to be revised accordingly to the top of the care-aid training. You should also consider, Mrs Minister – and this is a long time overdue – that there is at last your own professional image and professional protection for these groups of persons, because this is still missing and we need it very urgently. However, we will nevertheless agree because in principle it is a positive thing. It should be extended and extended accordingly. I very much hope that it will not remain alone with what is now being decided. The EU adaptation to the Psychologists Act is okay, the recognition of the Psychotherapy Act is also appropriate. On the whole, we will vote in favour of Greens, not all points, but most of them. I would ask you, Madam Minister, to: Look at these food supplements and, above all, magnetic field therapy, because there are many people who spend a lot of money on nothing and nothing again! If one could use this money elsewhere, it would really be much more helpful to all. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2003-07-10-022-XXII-NRSITZ-00029_d7e28425	Mein Ladies and gentlemen! Before closing the sitting, I would like to address a few brief words to you. It is the first half-year in which I have the honour and the responsibility to hold this House together with the Presidential Conference. We are in Parliament at the end of the first half of the year. After the government formation, which meant intensive work for all political groups until the month of February, we continued our intensive work in Parliament. We have held 27 plenary or assignment meetings as well as two special sessions, 62 laws and a federal Constitutional Act, concluded and approved 20 state contracts and two Article 15a contracts. 632 requests – I have seen 712 now – have been made, 81 committee meetings and 20 subcommittee meetings have been held. Ladies and gentlemen! We are a working parliament – and we do not have to be ashamed of our work! I would like to thank you for the consensual atmosphere, because, despite all the intense debates – and I am also very pleased that today's debate on the urgent question in all force of the positions has not been degenerated into a mud battle, or as always we have sometimes experienced it, but has been led fairly – we have, as has been said, a consensus atmosphere. More than 60 percent of the laws are passed unanimously! There is a good working atmosphere in the Presidential Conference, and I would like to expressly contact President Dr Heinz Fischer and Dipl.-Ing. Thomas Principal thank you for accompanying me as a newcomer in the presidential conference with advice and action. I can always rely on them on questions of the Rules of Procedure. Thank you very much! [[General condolences.]] Ladies and gentlemen! We should have a common concern: to preserve and protect the reputation of this House and the reputation of Members. This House is a fishing point for democracy. If it is made bad, we are made bad, this harms democracy. It is an alarm signal to me that today an OGM survey with the following objective data has been launched on the market. On the question: “Do Members work as much as comparable professional groups?” said 40 percent, yes, they work the same amount, 14 percent said they work more, so 54 percent of the population say they work the same amount or more. 34 per cent believe, however, that MEPs are working less. But tomorrow's media expenditure says: ‘Austrian doubt the work of the Members.’ This reflects a prejudice toward us, in which we sometimes participate ourselves. But I think we should be proud of our work. I know only Members with great commitment, with great expertise and real commitment. I believe that we should all have an interest in maintaining the opening up of this House, as it has started in recent years, and thus promoting greater transparency. I also believe that, in the end, the television broadcasts, which have been intensified, contribute to seeing that we are working. So I mean, we all have this common interest. Finally, I would like to particularly thank the members of the Presidential Conference, especially the four club directors, the so-called small presidential conference; without it, our work would not work so well. I would also like to thank all the Members of the House, ladies and gentlemen, and I would like to thank the staff in the clubs and, above all, the staff of the House. [[General condolences.]] We have had 8 percent of all drafts in so-called electronic law last year, i.e. in E-Law format, automated, paperless Parliament. The employees of the house – very few people are active here – have managed to increase this percentage to 70 percent. We are therefore very close to our objectives.
